# Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models

Dane Malenfant
Affiliation: School of Computer Science
Affiliation: McGill University
Affiliation: Mila - The Québec AI Institute
Email: [dane.malenfant@mail.mcgill.ca](mailto:)

###### Abstract

Cooperation can fail when socially valuable effort is costly, hard to observe, and benefits mainly someone else. Building on Holmström’s model of moral hazard in teams, the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game instantiates this hidden-action structure as a textual environment for language agents. An agent chooses between keeping an immediate local reward and paying a query cost to reveal a hidden safety fact that helps another agent’s downstream decision. We evaluate fourteen open-weight and four frontier models using measures of information acquisition, communication, downstream use, and team success. In matched 3,015-decision-per-model experiments, GPT-5.6 Sol, Claude Opus 4.8, and Nemotron-3 Ultra track the derived private-share boundary across nine query costs, with mean absolute errors of 0.013, 0.030, and 0.024. Muse Spark 1.1 responds directionally, whereas Fable 5 remains query-saturated. SFT, RLOO, SFT+RLOO, and GEPA produce heterogeneous mechanism changes. GEPA raises Muse team success from 22.2% to 100.0% while reducing query use from 51.1% to 0.3%. Frozen-prompt interventions show that this success depends on a learned rank–label mapping rather than direct revelation: changing the mapping reduces team success from 100.0% to 12.5% and then 0.0%. We introduce CREDIT (Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer), a mechanism-aligned multi-agent prompt-optimization algorithm that uses matched hidden-state twins and total-action replay to reward robust causal contribution rather than query frequency. Across five models and multiple seeds, CREDIT preserves query-mediated behavior while revealing model-specific acquisition and downstream-use bottlenecks. Optimization can reach the same aggregate outcome through direct revelation or a learned effective information structure, motivating mechanism-level evaluation and optimization rather than team success alone.

| --- |
|  |

## 1 Introduction

Language agents act through responses, tool calls, warnings, and downstream decisions ([Russell, 2010](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib33)). In multi-agent systems, safety-relevant actions such as verifying a claim, inspecting a plan, or flagging a risk consume tokens, latency, and compute while often benefiting another agent or the system as a whole. Will an agent take a socially valuable action when it alone pays the cost and receives little individual credit?

Holmström’s theory of moral hazard in teams formalizes this tension ([Alchian & Demsetz, 1972](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib3); [Holmstrom, 1982](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib12)). Agents choose costly efforts $e\_{i}$, and the team receives collective output $Y(e\_{1},\ldots,e\_{n})$. Effort can be socially worthwhile, $\frac{\partial Y}{\partial e\_{i}}>c\_{i}^{\prime}(e\_{i})$, yet privately unattractive when the agent receives only a share of team output, $\alpha\_{i}\frac{\partial Y}{\partial e\_{i}}<c\_{i}^{\prime}(e\_{i})$. Here, *moral hazard* denotes this hidden-action incentive wedge. The main experiment identifies its marginal-incentive prediction, while Appendix [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A2 "Appendix B Analytical Equilibrium Characterization of the Controlled Work-Stage Game ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") derives the Nash equilibria of the corresponding controlled strategic work-stage subgame.

Aggregate team success obscures whether information was acquired, communicated, or used and whether success required direct revelation, so stage-level metrics are needed to distinguish mechanism execution, stage-specific failure, and learned changes to the effective information structure. The Dialogue Moral Hazard Game instantiates this incentive wedge as a choice between local payoff and costly revelation for another agent: its query–warning–decision chain separates acquisition, communication, downstream use, and team success. Because final success does not require that path, optimization may instead learn predictive regularities that change querying’s value.

##### Contributions.

This paper makes three contributions.

- •

  A controlled paradigm. The Dialogue Moral Hazard Game separates costly information acquisition, public communication, downstream use, and team outcome, and supports matched incentive and information-structure interventions across fourteen open-weight checkpoints and four frontier models.
- •

  Two information regimes. Under a fixed query mechanism, API-served policies can track the Holmström private-incentive boundary—GPT-5.6 Sol, Claude Opus 4.8, and Nemotron-3 Ultra within mean absolute errors of 0.013, 0.030, and 0.024. Diagnostic adaptation exposes stage-specific preservation and repair, whereas outcome-directed GEPA can replace direct revelation with an observable predictor. Freezing optimized prompts and intervening on the rank–label mapping identifies which information channel each policy depends on.
- •

  Mechanism-aligned optimization. Motivated by cue competition and blocking ([Kamin, 1969](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib17); [Mackintosh, 1975](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib25)), CREDIT uses matched hidden-state twins and counterfactual net-VOI credit to score query trajectories by their marginal contribution rather than by aggregate success or query frequency. Across three optimization seeds each for Qwen3-4B IT, OLMo-7B, Gemma 4B, Muse Spark 1.1, and Claude Opus 4.8, it yields query-mediated policies that persist across all three mappings. Muse and Opus complete the pathway at ceiling, while downstream information use remains a bottleneck for the open-weight models.

Appendix [A.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A1.SS4 "A.4 Positioning Relative to Cooperative Games ‣ Appendix A Background and Related Work ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") positions the task relative to cooperative games, and Appendix [C](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") specifies statistical units, seeds, and configurations.1

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7/images/dialogue_moral_hazard_game_diagram.png)


Figure 1: One successful episode. An agent either preserves local reward or pays to reveal another case’s hidden hazard, posts an anonymous warning, and enables the downstream safe choice.

## 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests

### 2.1 Dialogue Moral Hazard Game

The Dialogue Moral Hazard Game is a finite-horizon textual game designed to isolate costly, other-benefiting effort. Each agent owns a case with visible action utilities and one hidden unsafe option that only another agent can reveal, and only by paying a query cost. Play proceeds through observation, work, note, and final stages. At the work stage an agent either preserves a local payoff opportunity or queries another case; a querying agent may then communicate the revealed fact; and every agent finally selects an action. This staging separates private effort from communication and from downstream use. Figure [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Contributions. ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") illustrates a complete two-agent episode across these stages.

##### Formal game and oracle.

Let $N=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ be agents arranged in a directed ring, with successor $\sigma(i)=(i\bmod n)+1$. Agent $i$ owns case $i$, with options $A\_{i}$, public utilities $u\_{i}:A\_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$, and one hidden unsafe option $h\_{i}\in A\_{i}$. The target final action is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$a\_{i}^{\*}=\operatorname\*{arg\,max}\_{a\in A\_{i}\setminus\{h\_{i}\}}u\_{i}(a),\qquad T(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{h})=\prod\_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[a\_{i}=a\_{i}^{\*}],$$ |  | (1) |

where $T=1$ denotes team success. During the work stage, $e\_{i}=1$ privately reveals $h\_{\sigma(i)}$ to agent $i$ at cost $k$, whereas $e\_{i}=0$ permits a local answer instead. Because the ring is directed, this holds for any $n>2$ as well: every query targets exactly one successor case, and every case can be informed by exactly one predecessor. During the note stage, a querying agent may post NOTE CASE <ID> UNSAFE <OPTION> to a public board; a note is correct only when it names case $\sigma(i)$ and option $h\_{\sigma(i)}$. Provenance is hidden in the primary anonymous condition and shown only in the provenance ablation (Appendix [G](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A7 "Appendix G Provenance Visibility Across Baseline and Adapted Policies ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). During the final stage, each agent chooses $a\_{i}\in A\_{i}$ after observing the board.

##### Identifiable information intervention.

By design, the primary generator makes the highest-public-utility option the hidden unsafe label. This population-level relationship is not stated in any individual agent observation; however, observable text exposes utility ranks, and scored optimization episodes can reveal their relationship to hidden labels across episodes. The *balanced* intervention decorrelates unsafe rank from public utility, and the *reversed* intervention makes the lowest-public-utility option unsafe; visible templates and sampling streams are matched across all three mappings, and direct oracle revelation remains valid throughout. The rank–label relationship is thus an experimental manipulandum for identifying which learned policies use this cross-episode regularity, not an input to or requirement of the query mechanism.

##### Reward and credit.

Let $L\_{i}$ indicate that agent $i$ chose the local action and correctly answered its public local question, $F\_{i}=\mathbf{1}[a\_{i}=a\_{i}^{\*}]$, and $Q\_{i}=e\_{i}$. The implemented episode score is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$R\_{\mathrm{game}}=w\_{L}\frac{1}{n}\sum\_{i=1}^{n}L\_{i}+w\_{F}\frac{1}{n}\sum\_{i=1}^{n}F\_{i}+w\_{T}T-k\frac{1}{n}\sum\_{i=1}^{n}Q\_{i},$$ |  | (2) |

with primary values $(w\_{L},w\_{F},w\_{T},k)=(0.35,0.15,0.50,0.10)$. Querying costs $k$ and forfeits any chance of earning $L\_{i}$, while the all-or-nothing team component grants no marginal credit to an individual query or note. This score serves as both the common evaluation payoff and the GEPA objective; the distinct SFT and RLOO signals are specified in Appendix [C](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"). Here, *utility* denotes a visible action value $u\_{i}(a)$ and *reward* a realized evaluation or training score, so a high-utility action can still be team-harmful whenever it happens to be the hidden unsafe one.
Let $\ell\_{i}\in[0,1]$ denote the attainable local-correctness value under the local route. The total private opportunity cost of querying, measured in reward units, is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$\kappa\_{i}\equiv k+w\_{L}\ell\_{i},\qquad c\_{i}(e\_{i})=\kappa\_{i}e\_{i}.$$ |  | (3) |

Writing $Y(e\_{1},\ldots,e\_{n})$ for group output yields the discrete marginal comparison at the heart of the game: querying is socially worthwhile when $\Delta\_{i}Y>\kappa\_{i}$, yet can remain privately unattractive when the actor’s share satisfies $\alpha\_{i}\Delta\_{i}Y<\kappa\_{i}$. This comparison requires no linear production function.

Execution through the designated two-agent mechanism combines symmetric queries, useful warnings, and the correct use of received facts. Team success, by contrast, scores final-action correctness. A zero-query policy that selects the correct actions therefore earns team reward under the same contract, while the mechanism metrics identify that it did not execute direct revelation. Joint reporting of local reward, query rate, information transfer, unsafe choice, format validity, and team success traces the pathway from costly acquisition to collective completion without equating the outcome with mechanism execution. Exact metric and parser definitions appear in Appendix [C.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.SS2 "C.2 Metric and Parser Definitions ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

### 2.2 Construct Validity Through Incentive Response

Two complementary experiments characterize incentive response under the designated information structure at different levels of resolution. The main autonomous comparison keeps the complete query–warning–decision pipeline intact while varying query cost, thereby measuring the end-to-end response to the private-cost term. Complementary team-reward and group-size sweeps appear in Appendix [E](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5 "Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"). A private-share incentive-isolation experiment fixes partner behavior and varies query cost and the focal agent’s share of team output independently, isolating the Holmström-style private-incentive comparison.

Figure 2: API-served model query-cost sweeps: A GPT-5.6 Sol, B Muse Spark 1.1, C Fable 5, D Claude Opus 4.8, and E Nemotron-3 Ultra. Curves show mean query, team success, local reward, and unsafe choice; bands show $\pm 1$ SD over three 15-episode API replicates. The dashed line marks the coordinated-profile crossover, $k^{\mathrm{profile}}=0.30$; $w\_{T}=0.50$ and $n=2$.

Figure [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 2.2 Construct Validity Through Incentive Response ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") exercises the full autonomous pipeline. Under the primary weights, the coordinated all-query route is worth $w\_{F}+w\_{T}-k$ and the all-local route is worth $w\_{L}$, giving profile crossovers $k^{\mathrm{profile}}=w\_{T}^{\mathrm{profile}}=0.30$. This route comparison is distinct from the unilateral all-query Nash cutoff $k^{\mathrm{NE}}=0.15$ derived in Appendix [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A2 "Appendix B Analytical Equilibrium Characterization of the Controlled Work-Stage Game ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"). Sol’s query rate falls from $98.9\pm 1.9\%$ at $k=0.15$ to $4.4\pm 1.9\%$ at $k=0.40$, while local-reward preservation rises from $1.1\pm 1.9\%$ to $94.4\pm 1.9\%$; at the coordinated-profile crossover $k=0.30$, query rate is $70.0\pm 8.8\%$.

Muse is also cost-sensitive: from $k=0.00$ to $k=0.40$, query rate falls from $76.7\pm 5.8\%$ to $3.3\pm 3.3\%$, team success falls from $62.2\pm 3.8\%$ to $0.0\pm 0.0\%$, and unsafe choice rises from $14.4\pm 1.9\%$ to $36.7\pm 3.3\%$. At $k^{\mathrm{profile}}=0.30$, Muse queries in $7.8\pm 3.8\%$ of episodes. Unlike Sol, Muse does not switch cleanly to a valid local-reward policy: local reward remains $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ throughout, while format validity falls from $72.2\pm 6.9\%$ to $3.3\pm 3.3\%$. Fable instead shifts to local work well before the coordinated-profile crossover. Its query rate falls from $55.6\pm 1.9\%$ at $k=0.00$ to $5.6\pm 1.9\%$ at $k=0.15$ and $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ at $k=0.40$, while local reward rises from $42.2\pm 1.9\%$ to $100.0\pm 0.0\%$. Team success remains $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ at every cost.

Opus executes a valid switch between the same routes. Its query rate declines from $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ at $k=0.00$ to $45.6\pm 9.6\%$ at $k^{\mathrm{profile}}=0.30$ and $13.3\pm 3.3\%$ at $k=0.40$; local reward rises from $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ to $86.7\pm 3.3\%$, while format validity remains $100\%$. Team success correspondingly falls from $93.3\pm 0.0\%$ to $0.0\pm 0.0\%$. Thus Sol and Opus transition near the coordinated-profile crossover through valid query-to-local policies, while Muse’s transition is entangled with validity and Fable switches earlier.

Nemotron-3 Ultra exhibits a noisier but directional cost response: query use falls from $48.9\pm 7.7\%$ at $k=0$ to $20.0\pm 3.3\%$ at $k^{\mathrm{profile}}=0.30$ and $24.4\pm 9.6\%$ at $k=0.40$. Local-reward preservation does not rise correspondingly, and format validity falls from $68.9\pm 1.9\%$ to $43.3\pm 8.8\%$, separating reduced acquisition from a clean switch to the local route. Complementary team-reward sweeps for all five API-served models, together with matched Sol, Muse, Opus, and Nemotron group-size sweeps through $n=10$, appear in Appendix [E](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5 "Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), Figure [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ E.1 Autonomous Payoff and Group-Size Sweeps ‣ Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

##### Private-share incentive isolation.

Partner behavior is fixed to isolate the incentive margin, yielding the focal agent’s private payoff

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$U\_{i}=w\_{L}L\_{i}+w\_{F}F\_{i}+n\alpha\_{i}w\_{T}T-kQ\_{i},$$ |  | (4) |

where $\alpha\_{i}$ is the focal agent’s private share of the fixed team-success value. Because shares sum to one, varying $\alpha\_{i}$ redistributes private credit while leaving the social score untouched. Scripted partners guarantee that $F\_{i}=1$ under either first-step action, that query yields $T=1$, and that local yields $T=0$. The focal comparison therefore reduces exactly to

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$m\_{i}\equiv U\_{i}(\textsc{query})-U\_{i}(\textsc{local})=n\alpha\_{i}w\_{T}-w\_{L}-k.$$ |  | (5) |

In this scripted comparison the local question is fully attainable, so $\ell\_{i}=1$ and Equation [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E3 "In Reward and credit. ‣ 2.1 Dialogue Moral Hazard Game ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") gives $\kappa\_{i}=w\_{L}+k$. With $n=2$, $w\_{L}=0.35$, and $w\_{T}=0.50$, setting the private team benefit $n\alpha\_{i}w\_{T}$ equal to $\kappa\_{i}$ gives the predicted private indifference boundary

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$\alpha^{\*}(k)=\frac{w\_{L}+k}{nw\_{T}}=0.35+k.$$ |  | (6) |

Below this boundary the query remains socially beneficial but privately dominated; above it, private and social incentives align in favor of querying. Appendix [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A2 "Appendix B Analytical Equilibrium Characterization of the Controlled Work-Stage Game ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") embeds this $p=1$ boundary in the strategic best-response correspondence and characterizes the resulting pure and symmetric mixed equilibria.

Figure 3: Private-share incentive isolation. A: Predicted boundary $\alpha^{\*}(k)=0.35+k$ and empirical 50% thresholds for GPT-5.6 Sol, Claude Opus 4.8, and Nemotron-3 Ultra (MAE 0.013, 0.030, and 0.024); bands and whiskers are paired-cluster bootstrap 95% CIs. Muse and Fable never cross 50%. B: Query rates at $m\_{i}=-0.05,0,+0.05$ with Wilson 95% CIs; each bar pools 405 decisions. Full sweeps appear in Appendix [E](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5 "Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

Figure [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Private-share incentive isolation. ‣ 2.2 Construct Validity Through Incentive Response ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") identifies three boundary-tracking policies. Sol’s empirical threshold follows the prediction across all nine query costs with mean absolute error 0.013, and its pooled query rate moves from $0.0\%$ immediately below indifference to $68.9\%$ at the boundary and $100.0\%$ immediately above it. Opus has mean absolute error 0.030 and corresponding rates of $14.8\%$, $100.0\%$, and $100.0\%$. Nemotron independently supplies a second global transition: its three-replicate thresholds have mean absolute error 0.024, and query use moves from $0/405$ immediately below indifference to $11/405$ at indifference and $404/405$ immediately above it.

Muse and Fable remain above 50% querying throughout the sampled grid and therefore have no estimable boundary. Muse nevertheless responds directionally: its high-share-minus-low-share contrast is $+23.1$ points (95% bootstrap CI $[18.8,27.7]$). Fable remains near saturation, with a $+4.7$-point share contrast ($[2.7,6.9]$) and a $-1.8$-point cost contrast whose interval crosses zero ($[-4.4,0.4]$), despite its pronounced autonomous cost response. Full cells, maximum boundary errors, controls, and matched contrasts appear in Appendix [E](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5 "Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

##### Payoff reasoning and objective priority.

Mathematical payoff reasoning supports utility optimization by relating action costs to private and collective benefits. Instrumental rationality is relative to the specified objective: below the private-share boundary, maximizing $U\_{i}$ favors local, even though query remains socially beneficial. Accurate arithmetic therefore supports incentive-sensitive choice but does not itself imply cooperation. The private-share experiment separates comprehension of the payoff ordering from acting on it: comprehension is $100.0\%$ for Sol, Muse, Fable, and Opus, and $99.7\%$ for Nemotron (Table [23](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5.T23 "Table 23 ‣ E.2 Private-Share Incentive Isolation ‣ Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). Boundary error measures behavioral adherence to the explicit private-payoff comparison, rather than general mathematical ability or strategic reasoning.

Departures from that boundary are consequently substantive behavioral differences. Fable and Opus query in $97.9\%$ and $46.3\%$ of privately dominated decisions, respectively, despite perfect payoff comprehension. Their visible rationales sometimes acknowledge the private loss and prioritize avoiding the successor’s unsafe choice (Table [25](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5.T25 "Table 25 ‣ E.3 Qualitative Analysis of Visible Private-Share Rationales ‣ Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"); Appendix [E.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A5.SS3 "E.3 Qualitative Analysis of Visible Private-Share Rationales ‣ Appendix E API-Served Model Incentive and Private-Share Sweeps ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). These records are consistent with safety-prioritizing policies rather than simple arithmetic mistakes. Opus’s small boundary error describes a local switch embedded in this broader policy. Thus payoff comprehension, adherence to the assigned private objective, and willingness to cooperate are distinct properties.

### 2.3 Learning Interventions and Effective Information

The autonomous and private-share incentive-response tests hold the designated information structure fixed. Adaptation can preserve or repair that query mechanism, but it can also change the information available to the policy. Four diagnostic interventions expose these possibilities under deliberately distinct objectives. SFT imitates scripted query–warning–decision trajectories; REINFORCE leave-one-out (RLOO) uses leave-one-out policy gradients to reward valid rationale structure and target-action matching ([Williams, 1992](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib39); [Ahmadian et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib2)); SFT+RLOO applies that refinement after supervised initialization; and GEPA optimizes natural-language prompts against $R\_{\mathrm{game}}$ through DSPy ([Khattab et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib18); [Agrawal et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib1)). The weight-level interventions use LoRA ([Hu et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib13)). Identical held-out game metrics provide behavioral contrasts across their distinct objectives and policy substrates rather than a matched-objective optimizer comparison. CREDIT (Section [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.SS4 "2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")) is a new mechanism-aligned optimization algorithm with its own counterfactual training distribution, replay procedure, robust causal-credit objective, candidate-selection order, and stage-transition rule. GEPA serves only as a replaceable generator of candidate natural-language instructions.

Under GEPA, the evaluated model acts as both task policy and reflection model. Between optimization rounds it reviews minibatches of its own redacted reasoning and action traces, each paired with a scalar score, and proposes revised instructions. This across-episode self-reflection searches over natural-language policies beyond the hand-specified query sequence, admitting both query-mediated cooperation and predictive decision policies. Table [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.T6 "Table 6 ‣ C.3 Intervention Configurations ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), the formal objectives, and all launch configurations appear in Appendix [C](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

##### Endogenous effective information.

*Predictive semantics* denotes a policy-relevant statistical mapping from observable textual features to the conditional distribution of a hidden task variable. The balanced and reversed interventions measure whether each learned mapping persists when the rank–label relationship changes.
Let $x\_{i}$ denote the observable text and let $\psi$ denote a learned policy. The relevant query margin after learning is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$m\_{i}(x\_{i};\psi)=\mathbb{E}\_{\psi}[U\_{i}\mid Q\_{i}=1,x\_{i}]-\mathbb{E}\_{\psi}[U\_{i}\mid Q\_{i}=0,x\_{i}].$$ |  | (7) |

Under the designated mechanism, query reveals the hidden unsafe option, and the controlled experiment collapses this margin to Equation [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E5 "In Private-share incentive isolation. ‣ 2.2 Construct Validity Through Incentive Response ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"). But because GEPA sees only scalar scores and redacted traces on disjoint optimization episodes, any such predictive relationship learned over $x\_{i}$ raises the no-query term in Equation [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E7 "In Endogenous effective information. ‣ 2.3 Learning Interventions and Effective Information ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), thereby altering both the effective information structure and the marginal value of querying.

### 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer

Standard GEPA is outcome-directed: it can improve $R\_{\mathrm{game}}$ by strengthening the query–warning–decision pathway or by learning an observable predictor that makes querying unnecessary. The latter creates predictive-semantics misalignment: aggregate reward improves while the optimized policy bypasses the designated information-acquisition mechanism, as the frozen-prompt mapping interventions of Section [3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.SS3 "3.3 In-Context Learning Changes the Effective Information Structure ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") demonstrate directly. *CREDIT* (Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer) is a new mechanism-aligned multi-agent prompt-optimization algorithm for the complete acquisition–communication–use pathway. CREDIT defines a counterfactual update operator that constructs matched hidden-state training worlds, replays complete query and no-query trajectories, assigns robust opportunity-cost-adjusted causal credit, and controls candidate selection through an acquisition–communication–use state machine. It orders policies using controlled mechanism scores and a joint feasibility-banded score containing statewise net-VOI credit. CREDIT changes the training distribution, replay procedure, credit assignment, candidate-selection order, and stage transitions while leaving the acting agents’ observations, action grammar, utilities, query cost, and held-out game reward unchanged.

CREDIT maps a visible template and candidate policy to a counterfactual training case, replay graph, robust credit signal, feasibility decision, and stage transition. These quantities are mutually dependent: the matched worlds determine the replayed alternatives, their complete trajectories determine net-VOI and worst-state feasibility, and those decisions determine which prompt component advances to the next stage. GEPA supplies self-reflective natural-language candidate proposals; CREDIT independently defines the training evidence, replay graph, credit assignment, robustness criterion, selection objective, and curriculum. Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") gives the complete update; Appendix [C.3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.SS3.SSS2 "C.3.2 Component Identification ‣ C.3 Intervention Configurations ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") maps its operations to their empirical diagnostics.

##### Psychological motivation.

CREDIT draws structural motivation from cue competition, counterfactual reasoning, instrumental information seeking, and staged discrimination learning. These analogies determine the intervention structure while leaving the language model’s cognitive implementation unspecified. In blocking, a cue that already predicts reinforcement attenuates learning about an added cue that contributes little new predictive information ([Kamin, 1969](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib17); [Mackintosh, 1975](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib25)). Here, repeatedly rewarded utility-rank patterns can dominate reflective prompt search while the costly query pathway receives weak credit. Matched hidden-state twins act as an unblocking and cue-validity intervention: they hold public cues fixed while changing the hidden unsafe option and correct action, so the observable cue cannot solve every member ([Brunswik, 1955](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib6); [Kahneman & Tversky, 1973](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib16)). Total-action replay then supplies a functional counterfactual comparison between the observed trajectory and matched query or local alternatives ([Roese, 1994](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib32)). Net-VOI credit treats information seeking as instrumental: querying receives credit when it changes downstream behavior enough to exceed its private costs ([Gottlieb et al., 2013](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib9)). Finally, the curriculum isolates acquisition, communication, and use before removing those supports and requiring the complete on-policy pathway, analogous to staged discrimination learning ([Terrace, 1963](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib36)).

##### Matched hidden-state twins.

For each visible episode template $x$, let $\mathcal{H}(x)$ be the complete Cartesian set of decision-relevant hidden assignments over the two highest-utility options in every case. Public utilities, option order, local task, agent identities, payoff contract, and pre-query prompts are identical within the set; only the hidden unsafe options and therefore the correct safe actions change. With two agents, each template has four twins. Decoding streams are matched within each set. A fixed observable-only rule cannot solve every member because identical visible inputs require different actions. Matched twins therefore create decision-relevant epistemic uncertainty: the same observable state is compatible with hidden states that require different decisions. CREDIT consequently aggregates performance by the worst hidden state rather than allowing a shortcut-compatible twin to compensate for failure on its matched counterpart. Replay assigns credit precisely when resolving that uncertainty improves downstream reward after private costs.

##### Counterfactual contribution and net information value.

For actor $i$ and hidden state $h$, CREDIT replays complete first-step alternatives. Let $\tau\_{i}^{Q}(x,h)$ be the trajectory generated by query, including its cost, the emitted information record, and the beneficiary’s downstream decision. Let $\tau\_{i}^{a}(x,h)$ denote the matched continuation after valid local action $a$, with descendants that require a query removed and only affected downstream stages regenerated. The net value of information is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$\operatorname{VOI}\_{i}(x,h)=R\_{\mathrm{game}}\!\left(\tau\_{i}^{Q}(x,h)\right)-\max\_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{L}\_{i}}R\_{\mathrm{game}}\!\left(\tau\_{i}^{a}(x,h)\right).$$ |  | (8) |

This comparison includes both the explicit query cost and the local reward forfeited by querying, together with any changes in final correctness and team success. The robust contribution credit is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$C\_{i}^{\operatorname{VOI}}(x)=\mathbf{1}\!\left[\min\_{h\in\mathcal{H}(x)}Q\_{i}(x,h)=1\right]\max\!\left\{0,\min\_{h\in\mathcal{H}(x)}\operatorname{VOI}\_{i}(x,h)\right\}.$$ |  | (9) |

This credit requires querying in every twin and a positive reward advantage over the best local replay in every hidden state. It is a clipped minimum of paired gains, not the difference between worst-case query and no-query rewards. A query can therefore improve worst-case reward while receiving zero credit if a no-query continuation is at least as good in another state. Harmful information events are recorded but cannot become positive contribution credit.

In the final joint stage, let $[z]\_{0}^{1}=\max(0,\min(z,1))$. The reward-and-credit component for candidate prompt $q$ is

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | $$R\_{\mathrm{CREDIT}}(x;q)=\left[\frac{\min\_{h\in\mathcal{H}(x)}[R\_{\mathrm{game}}(x,h;q)]\_{0}^{1}+b\frac{1}{n}\sum\_{i}C\_{i}^{\operatorname{VOI}}(x;q)}{1+b}\right]\_{0}^{1},\qquad b=1.$$ |  | (10) |

The game-score clipping in Equation [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E10 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") is used for selection; Equation [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E8 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") retains raw game-reward differences. The optimizer maps this component and the mechanism diagnostics into a per-template banded score $S\_{\mathrm{joint}}(x;q)$, then averages that score over the validation templates. Earlier bands reward progress through format validity, contribution eligibility, information use, and positive-credit floors; Equation [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E10 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") orders candidates within the highest band. The joint stopping rule acts on the mean banded score, not directly on $R\_{\mathrm{CREDIT}}$, and does not require every validation template to pass the positive-credit floor. Appendix [C.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.SS3.SSS1 "C.3.1 CREDIT Optimization Protocol ‣ C.3 Intervention Configurations ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") gives the exact transformation and a worked example.

Algorithm 1  CREDIT update operator. GEPA supplies the Propose operation.

1:
Training templates $\mathcal{X}$; complete validation set $\mathcal{V}$; initial prompt program $q=(q^{W},q^{N},q^{F})$; proposer $\mathcal{P}$; ordered stages $\mathcal{S}$; stage budgets $B\_{s}$ and criteria $\tau\_{s}$

2:
Frozen prompt program $q^{\star}$

3:
for all stages $s\in\mathcal{S}$ in curriculum order do

4:
  Initialize stage incumbent $q\_{s}\leftarrow q$

5:
  $\bar{s}\_{s}(q\_{s})\leftarrow\textsc{Validate}(q\_{s},\mathcal{V},s)$ $\triangleright$ score inherited program first

6:
  while $B\_{s}$ remains and $\bar{s}\_{s}(q\_{s})<\tau\_{s}$ do

7:
   Sample reflection minibatch $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{X}$; $\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\varnothing$

8:
   for all $x\in\mathcal{B}$ do

9:
     Construct matched hidden-state twins $\mathcal{H}(x)$ with identical visible inputs

10:
     for all $h\in\mathcal{H}(x)$ and actor $i$ do

11:
      Replay $\tau\_{i}^{Q}(x,h)$ and every valid $\tau\_{i}^{a}(x,h)$ under the controls for stage $s$

12:
      Compute $\operatorname{VOI}\_{i}(x,h)$ by Equation [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E8 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")

13:
     Compute robust contribution $C\_{i}^{\operatorname{VOI}}(x)$ by Equation [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E9 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")

14:
     Add trajectories, exact targets, and robust replay diagnostics to $\mathcal{E}$

15:
   $\mathcal{Q}\leftarrow\textsc{Propose}(\mathcal{P},q\_{s},s,\mathcal{E})$

16:
   for all candidate $\widetilde{q}\in\mathcal{Q}$ do

17:
     $\mathcal{D}\_{\widetilde{q}}\leftarrow\textsc{TwinReplay}(\widetilde{q},\mathcal{V},s)$

18:
     $\bar{s}\_{s}(\widetilde{q})\leftarrow\textsc{MeanStageScore}(s,\mathcal{D}\_{\widetilde{q}})$

19:
  *Stages 1–3:* controlled acquisition, exact communication, or correct information use

20:
  *Joint stage:* mean of per-template bands in Equation [27](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.E27 "In Implemented joint score. ‣ C.3.1 CREDIT Optimization Protocol ‣ C.3 Intervention Configurations ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")

21:
     if $\bar{s}\_{s}(\widetilde{q})>\bar{s}\_{s}(q\_{s})$ then

22:
      $q\_{s}\leftarrow\widetilde{q}$ $\triangleright$ accept candidate

23:
     else

24:
      Reject $\widetilde{q}$

25:
  $q\leftarrow q\_{s}$ $\triangleright$ advance after threshold attainment or budget exhaustion

26:
return $q^{\star}\leftarrow q$

##### Staged mechanism alignment.

End-to-end credit can hide acquisition’s value when an initial note prompt mistransmits the oracle result or an initial final prompt ignores a correct warning. CREDIT therefore follows a four-stage curriculum. Query discovery optimizes the work action under controlled correct communication and responsive downstream use; communication then isolates exact transmission of a forced query result; information use isolates the final decision under a canonical warning; and joint optimization removes these controls so all prompt components execute on-policy. Prompts learned in one stage initialize the next. Joint selection is feasibility-first within each template: validity, contribution eligibility, information use, and positive credit determine its score band before Equation [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E10 "In Counterfactual contribution and net information value. ‣ 2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") applies. The validation mean can trade improvements across templates, so this within-template ordering is distinct from requiring all templates to satisfy every floor.

Through its GEPA backend, the reflection model receives sampled traces and proposes replacement natural-language instructions for the active prompt component. CREDIT additionally supplies compact factual and action-replay diagnostics, including controlled query and local outcomes, exact information targets, beneficiary decisions with and without the information, and robust net value. These counterfactual targets are training-side evidence. Evaluation agents receive the frozen instructions and ordinary episode observations, not episode-specific counterfactual records. Selected prompts are evaluated with the unchanged game reward and decomposed mechanism metrics under original rank-correlated, balanced, and reversed environments. Appendix [C.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3.SS3.SSS1 "C.3.1 CREDIT Optimization Protocol ‣ C.3 Intervention Configurations ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") provides the complete curriculum, feasibility gates, optimization budget, early-stopping rule, data splits, and seed protocol.

## 3 Results

Every policy is scored with the same game payoff, parser, and mechanism metrics. In result tables, bold denotes the largest reported mean for open-weight and frontier models separately within each metric column; mean ties are resolved by lower standard deviation and then table order. *Base* denotes a released checkpoint evaluated before task-specific adaptation. Appendix [C](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocol ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") defines the model-specific experimental units, seeds, and configurations.

Figure 4: Base-policy geometry; markers denote models and the red $\times$ denotes the matched random baseline. A: Local reward versus team success, with Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.6 Sol in the inset. B: Query rate versus realized transfer; the dashed line marks equality, and points below it acquire more information than they transfer.

Figure [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3.1 Base Models Differ in Costly Cooperation ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") lays out a progression from local-reward preservation through acquisition to transfer. Qwen3-0.6B and SmolLM3-3B rarely query, whereas Gemma-2-2B IT and OLMo-7B query frequently but realize only $9.2\%$ and $11.2\%$ information transfer: acquisition and transfer are separable failures, and Fable shows the same dissociation while preserving local reward. The larger open-weight checkpoints do not collapse to one profile: OLMo3.1-32B reaches $27.3\pm 6.9\%$ team success through query-mediated transfer, whereas Qwen3.6-27B reaches $0.9\pm 0.4\%$ despite comparable scale. Nemotron-3 Ultra, a 550B-total/55B-active open-weight mixture-of-experts model evaluated through API, matches its query and transfer rates ([NVIDIA, 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#bib.bib26)). Mistral NeMo 12B is limited earlier in the pipeline, at $7.4\pm 0.9\%$ format validity, so its absent team success reflects protocol failure rather than a cooperation choice. Among frontier policies, only Sol executes the complete pathway at ceiling, with Opus close behind and Muse intermediate. The random control anchors chance performance (Appendix [I.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9.SS3 "I.3 Random Valid-Action Control ‣ Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). For OpenThinker3-7B and Qwen3.5-9B, a 32,768-token control sharply raises format validity without yielding any team success, separating protocol execution from cooperative decision quality (Appendix [I.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9.SS4 "I.4 Generation-Budget Control for Reasoning Models ‣ Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")).

Appendix [K](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A11 "Appendix K Descriptive Parameter-Scale Analysis ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") provides a descriptive parameter-scale analysis of fourteen open-weight checkpoints spanning $0.6$B–$55$B active parameters under base and GEPA policies; Nemotron-3 Ultra is plotted at its 55B active count (550B total). SFT, RLOO, and SFT+RLOO cover thirteen, eleven, and twelve checkpoints, respectively. Team success is not monotonically ordered by size under base or weight-adapted policies. GEPA outcomes have a positive descriptive size association, but the larger policies use distinct mechanisms—including zero-query prediction and near-complete revelation—so active parameter count does not determine how improvement is achieved.

### 3.2 Weight-Level Adaptation Across Mechanism Components

Released checkpoints differ not only in how often they succeed but in where along the pathway they stop. Matched intervention-minus-base changes identify which mechanism components become available under imitation, target-action reward, or their sequence. Because the objectives differ, these contrasts characterize policy responses rather than rank the underlying optimizers.

A

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7/training_delta_heatmap_exact_conditions.png)

B

Figure 5: Weight-level effects. A: Team-success changes (percentage points) by model and intervention. B: Mechanism profiles for OLMo-7B, SmolLM3-3B, and OLMo3.1-32B (top to bottom). Bars and whiskers show mean $\pm 1$ SD over three model-run seeds. Blank cells and groups denote conditions without estimates.

The update map in Figure [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 3.2 Weight-Level Adaptation Across Mechanism Components ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") exposes distinct, stage-specific trajectories. For SmolLM3-3B, SFT lifts team success from $0.9\pm 0.8\%$ to $64.7\pm 7.5\%$ and information transfer from $3.7\pm 0.5\%$ to $68.3\pm 11.0\%$; SFT+RLOO reaches the same $64.7\%$ team-success mean while shrinking the across-seed standard deviation from $7.5$ to $1.4$ points, whereas standalone RLOO stays near base. Qwen3-0.6B, by contrast, learns acquisition without the downstream pathway: SFT drives query rate from $0.4\pm 0.0\%$ to $74.5\pm 18.6\%$, yet transfer reaches only $6.0\pm 4.2\%$ and team success only $0.7\pm 1.3\%$.

OLMo-7B starts acquisition-dominant and undergoes coordinated pathway repair: SFT+RLOO raises information transfer from $11.2\pm 1.0\%$ to $62.0\pm 13.2\%$ and team success from $1.2\pm 0.3\%$ to $38.8\pm 13.1\%$. Mistral NeMo 12B instead shows smaller, stage-specific changes: SFT reaches $3.6\pm 3.1\%$ team success and $89.8\pm 12.2\%$ validity, standalone RLOO remains at $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ team success, and sequential SFT+RLOO reaches $1.9\pm 3.0\%$ team success with $3.9\pm 6.5\%$ transfer per opportunity. These profiles distinguish end-to-end trajectory acquisition, acquisition-only learning, repair of an existing query policy, and parser improvement without reliable team completion. Exact deltas and complete mechanism breakdowns appear in Appendix [I](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9 "Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

The larger checkpoints retain this heterogeneity. OLMo3.1-32B reaches $35.7\pm 17.0\%$ team success after SFT and $36.9\pm 17.1\%$ after SFT+RLOO, changes of $+8.4$ and $+9.6$ percentage points from base. These outcome gains do not coincide with higher aggregate transfer: the corresponding rates are $46.2\pm 19.2\%$ and $51.1\pm 17.0\%$, compared with $52.7\pm 8.4\%$ at base. Qwen3.6-27B SFT remains at $0.9\pm 0.4\%$ team success, with $9.8\pm 0.5\%$ transfer and $52.9\pm 2.9\%$ format validity. Larger capacity therefore does not uniformly improve the response to trajectory imitation or subsequent reward-based refinement.

### 3.3 In-Context Learning Changes the Effective Information Structure

The deliberately controlled rank–label relationship becomes a treatment variable. The initial optimization runs surface policies that act without direct revelation; freezing those policies and intervening on the relationship separates reliance on optimization-time predictive information from direct query-mediated acquisition.

Figure 6: GEPA policy geometry relative to base. A: Team-success change versus query-rate change. B: Team-success change versus local-reward change. Shared markers identify models across panels and separate query-mediated gains from gains accompanied by greater local-reward preservation. Points are three-prompt means. The Claude Opus 4.8 and Nemotron-3 Ultra means each combine one query-mediated and two near-zero-query prompts.

Figure [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 3.3 In-Context Learning Changes the Effective Information Structure ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") shows that GEPA changes both outcome and mechanism geometry. Local-reward preservation is not mechanically the complement of query rate: malformed or invalid outputs and incorrect local actions earn no local reward, so they contribute to neither measure. Panel B makes the return to local reward visible for several query-reducing policies; the frozen interventions below determine whether those shifts reflect usable predictive information.

Two distinct policy regimes emerge. Muse supplies the clearest within-model bridge: team success rises from $22.2\pm 3.8\%$ to $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ while query use falls from $51.1\pm 5.1\%$ to $0.3\pm 0.5\%$. Its three prompts independently learn the same observable rule—treat the highest-public-utility option as unsafe and choose the next-highest option—rather than strengthening the query–warning pathway. Opus reaches $100\%$ team success with three different prompts, but one queries on every opportunity while two almost never query. Nemotron independently reproduces this mechanism split: across three prompts it reaches $97.9\pm 2.4\%$ team success, but query use is $33.1\pm 57.3\%$ because one prompt queries and transfers information nearly perfectly while two use the public-utility predictor. Larger checkpoints sharpen the cross-model contrast: OLMo3.1-32B reaches $91.1\pm 5.5\%$ team success with $98.4\pm 2.1\%$ query use, whereas Qwen3.6-27B reaches $77.1\pm 14.2\%$ with no queries or transfer. Qwen3-4B IT supplies another zero-query profile, while Gemma 4B and Mistral NeMo 12B improve by querying more. Complete outcomes and mechanism metrics appear in Table [44](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9.T44 "Table 44 ‣ I.1 GEPA Policy Outcomes ‣ Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") and Appendix [I](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9 "Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"); fixed-policy mapping evaluations identify whether the new policies depend on direct revelation or the optimization-time rank relationship.

|  |  | Original | | Balanced | | Reversed | |

| Model | Policy | Team | Query | Team | Query | Team | Query |

Table 2: Frozen-prompt information-structure interventions. Original preserves the optimization-time rank–label mapping, Balanced decorrelates it, and Reversed inverts it. Frozen entries are mean $\pm$ SD across three independently optimized prompts. Original API Base references use three replicates; shifted Opus Base uses three matched runs. Lower-panel Qwen interactions use 10,000 hierarchical paired-bootstrap draws. Full profiles appear in Appendix [H](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A8 "Appendix H Frozen-Prompt Information-Structure Interventions ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

Table [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3 In-Context Learning Changes the Effective Information Structure ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") intervenes directly on the candidate source of learned information. Muse’s frozen no-query prompts fall from $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ team success under the original mapping to $12.5\pm 0.0\%$ after decorrelation and $0.0\pm 0.0\%$ after reversal, while validity remains $100\%$. The matched base policy reaches $89.1\%$ and $100.0\%$ team success under the two shifts, so their collapse reflects loss of the observable rank rule’s predictive value rather than malformed execution or an unsolvable environment.

Opus supplies a within-model mechanism control. Its query-mediated prompt remains at $100\%$ team success and transfer under both shifts, whereas each near-zero-query prompt follows the same $100.0\%\rightarrow 12.5\%\rightarrow 0.0\%$ trajectory as Muse. Matched base success is $62.5\pm 0.0\%$ under balanced assignment and $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ under reversal. Thus the aggregate Opus means combine one robust revelation policy with two mapping-sensitive predictive policies rather than one partially successful strategy; complete prompt-level profiles appear in Appendix [H.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A8.SS2 "H.2 Opus Prompt-Level Mechanism Contrast ‣ Appendix H Frozen-Prompt Information-Structure Interventions ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

Qwen3-4B IT remains near 1% query use across mappings. Its direct original-minus-balanced and original-minus-reversed GEPA–base interactions are $+9.6$ points (95% CI $[-7.0,31.0]$) and $+11.2$ points ($[-13.5,36.7]$), respectively. The point estimates favor the optimization-time relationship, while the intervals include zero and reveal heterogeneity across prompt instances. Seed-level trajectories identify several learned rules, and additional controls show interactions in both directions across models; Appendix [H](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A8 "Appendix H Frozen-Prompt Information-Structure Interventions ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") reports those trajectories, complete mechanism metrics, and paired intervals.

Together, these interventions show that optimized prompts can use observable task features as predictive information across held-out episodes. The episode-specific unsafe option is absent from the initial observation and GEPA feedback, which contains scalar reward and redacted traces. In Equation [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.E7 "In Endogenous effective information. ‣ 2.3 Learning Interventions and Effective Information ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), this learned information raises the value of acting without direct revelation; the query-only Holmström boundary then no longer fully describes the policy’s decision problem. Fixed-policy controls, complete frozen-prompt results, optimized programs, and objective-specific sensitivity analyses appear in Appendices [F](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A6 "Appendix F Deterministic Information-Structure Controls ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), [H](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A8 "Appendix H Frozen-Prompt Information-Structure Interventions ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), [N](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A14 "Appendix N GEPA-Optimized Prompts and Learned Information Policies ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models"), and [I.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A9.SS8 "I.8 Objective-Specific Effects and Sensitivity ‣ Appendix I Evaluation Controls and Statistical Details ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models").

### 3.4 CREDIT Recovers Query-Mediated Information Transfer

Mapping-dependent predictive policies motivate a distinct optimization target: reward direct revelation when it improves a matched outcome, rather than reward aggregate success or query frequency alone. CREDIT (Section [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S2.SS4 "2.4 CREDIT: Counterfactual Replay for Evidence-Driven Information Transfer ‣ 2 Experimental Framework and Construct-Validity Tests ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")) uses matched hidden-state twins to prevent one observable rank rule from solving every training case, while net-VOI credit rewards query trajectories for improving the matched continuation. Three independently optimized prompts for each of Qwen3-4B IT, OLMo-7B, Gemma 4B, Muse Spark 1.1, and Claude Opus 4.8 recover query-mediated policies without zero-query collapse.

Muse directly contrasts the two optimization targets. Standard GEPA achieves perfect original-mapping success through near-zero querying but falls to 12.5% and 0.0% success under decorrelation and reversal. Each of the three CREDIT prompts instead achieves 100% query use, realized transfer, and team success on all three mappings, with no unsafe choices and 100% format validity across 576 held-out seed–episode evaluations. On Opus, query use rises from 90.0% at base to $100.0\pm 0.0\%$, realized information transfer from 88.9% to $100.0\pm 0.0\%$, and team success from 77.8% to $100.0\pm 0.0\%$; all three frozen CREDIT prompts sustain the complete pathway across another 576 held-out seed–episode evaluations. Qwen remains selective, with $66.7\pm 14.5\%$ query use and approximately 50–56% realized transfer across mappings.

The open-weight models separate two roles for CREDIT: recovering an acquisition pathway and completing one that already exists. OLMo supplies a complementary acquisition-saturated profile: under the original rank-correlated environment, standard GEPA obtains $23.7\pm 32.0\%$ query use, $9.4\pm 12.1\%$ transfer, and $2.9\pm 3.7\%$ team success, whereas CREDIT obtains $99.0\pm 0.5\%$, $64.8\pm 5.9\%$, and $42.2\pm 6.8\%$, respectively. Its transfer rises to approximately 72–73% under balanced and reversed mappings, but team success remains 52–54%. Gemma serves as an acquisition-positive control: its frozen standard-GEPA prompts already sustain approximately 70% query use and 31–34% realized transfer across mappings. CREDIT therefore tests preservation and completion of an existing query-mediated pathway rather than acquisition discovery alone. Its query use remains approximately 92%, transfer rises from $56.3\pm 6.8\%$ to $63.3\pm 6.7\%$ to $70.3\pm 4.8\%$, and team success rises from $26.0\pm 8.9\%$ to $40.6\pm 8.3\%$ to $47.9\pm 6.3\%$.

Figure [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#S3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 3.4 CREDIT Recovers Query-Mediated Information Transfer ‣ 3 Results ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")A compares the two prompt-optimization methods directly under the original rank-correlated mapping. Relative to standard GEPA, CREDIT raises realized transfer for every model, by 25.7 points for Gemma at the low end and 99.7 for Muse at the high end. Thus every model moves toward more complete query-mediated cooperation, although residual communication and downstream-use errors remain for the open-weight policies.

Figure 7: CREDIT relative to standard GEPA and across frozen-policy information structures. Every model uses three prompts per method. A: Original-mapping seed means: open markers denote GEPA, filled markers CREDIT. Ellipse radii are one sample SD on each axis; zero spread collapses to a line or point. Muse and Opus CREDIT coincide at ceiling. B: CREDIT team success across mappings. C: Positive net-VOI—query opportunities whose query trajectory beats the best matched no-query continuation. Bands in B–C show $\pm 1$ sample SD. Table [65](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A13.T65 "Table 65 ‣ Appendix M CREDIT Evaluation and Optimization Dynamics ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") reports complete profiles.

Counterfactual replay separates mechanism execution from marginal contribution. Opus retains $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ query use and transfer while positive net-VOI falls from $100.0\pm 0.0\%$ to $25.0\pm 0.0\%$ to $0.0\pm 0.0\%$. Muse likewise retains perfect mechanism execution while positive net-VOI falls from $40.9\pm 1.6\%$ to $33.3\pm 0.5\%$ to $7.3\pm 1.8\%$. The open-weight policies also show declining positive net-VOI as the mapping changes (Table [65](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A13.T65 "Table 65 ‣ Appendix M CREDIT Evaluation and Optimization Dynamics ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). These lower rates indicate that matched no-query continuations increasingly reach the same outcome: executing the designated mechanism and that execution improving reward are separate properties. CREDIT generalizes beyond the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game to strategic matrix games spanning coordination, signaling, reciprocity, and resource preservation (Appendix [M.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A13.SS3 "M.3 Strategic Generalization of the CREDIT Curriculum ‣ Appendix M CREDIT Evaluation and Optimization Dynamics ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models")). Appendix [M](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A13 "Appendix M CREDIT Evaluation and Optimization Dynamics ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") reports complete profiles, optimization dynamics, and efficiency; Appendix [O](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.23982v7#A15 "Appendix O CREDIT Optimized Prompts ‣ N.17 GPT-5.6 Sol ‣ N.16 Claude Opus 4.8 ‣ N.15 Muse Spark 1.1 ‣ N.14 Qwen3.6-27B ‣ N.13 Qwen3.5-9B ‣ GEPA seed 2. ‣ GEPA seed 1. ‣ GEPA seed 0. ‣ N.12 Qwen3-4B IT ‣ N.11 Qwen3-0.6B ‣ N.10 SmolLM3-3B ‣ N.9 OpenThinker3-7B ‣ N.8 OLMo3.1-32B ‣ N.7 OLMo-7B ‣ N.6 Nemotron-3 Ultra ‣ N.5 Mistral NeMo 12B ‣ N.4 Granite 3.3-8B ‣ N.3 Gemma 4B ‣ N.2 Gemma-2-2B IT ‣ N.1 Command R7B ‣ Appendix N GEPA-Optimized Prompts and Learned Information Policies ‣ Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models") preserves and analyzes the fifteen frozen programs.

## 4 Discussion

The experiments connect incentive response, effective information, and mechanism-aligned optimization through the information available to the policy. The private-share intervention separates understanding an incentive from following it. Sol and Nemotron track the explicit private-payoff boundary globally; Opus combines local tracking with safety-prioritizing departures, while Muse and Fable remain more query-prone despite near-ceiling payoff comprehension. Mathematical competence can support private utility maximization, but cooperation additionally depends on which objective governs action. Under conflicting incentives, better adherence to the private contract can reduce socially beneficial querying. The autonomous sweeps then test execution of the full cooperative pathway, separating a valid switch between query and local work from reduced querying caused by protocol failure.

Adaptation then changes where a policy lies in the same acquisition–communication–use decomposition. Weight-level interventions can learn the complete trajectory, acquire information without transferring it, or repair downstream stages of an existing query policy. Outcome-directed GEPA exposes a further distinction: some prompts retain direct revelation across mapping shifts, whereas others use an observable rank-based predictor whose value changes under the balanced and reversed interventions. The large open-weight checkpoints occupy both regimes, so capacity alone does not determine whether optimization preserves or replaces the designated mechanism.

CREDIT addresses the mechanism-selection problem identified by those interventions. By changing the training evidence and credit assignment rather than the held-out task, it increases query-mediated transfer relative to standard GEPA across all five evaluated models while allowing selective acquisition. Muse supplies the clearest between-objective contrast: the original outcome is perfect under either optimizer, but only its CREDIT policies preserve direct revelation and success across the mapping shifts. The open-weight policies remain limited by communication and downstream information use, which the stage-level metrics localize. Counterfactual replay adds a separate causal distinction: a policy may execute the query pathway even when the matched no-query continuation would reach the same outcome.

Holmström’s comparison is therefore conditional on the effective information structure. Once learning supplies predictive information from observable text, the no-query value shifts and the original private-cost comparison no longer fully characterizes the decision problem. Aggregate reward alone cannot distinguish that change from direct revelation; stage-level measurement and counterfactual contribution credit can.

### 4.1 Limitations

The paradigm fixes private information, effort cost, communication access, and payoff accounting to support stage-level attribution and incentive-boundary tests.

Thirteen open-weight checkpoints spanning 0.6B–32B receive matched multi-seed base and GEPA evaluation; Nemotron-3 Ultra adds three-replicate base and private-share results and three GEPA seeds at 550B total/55B active parameters. SFT, RLOO, and SFT+RLOO cover thirteen, eleven, and twelve checkpoints, with eleven shared across all three conditions. Fable 5, Muse Spark 1.1, Claude Opus 4.8, and GPT-5.6 Sol extend the behavioral tests to frontier API policies. The analysis concerns observable policies under explicit reward contracts.

The CREDIT evaluation contains three optimization seeds each for Qwen3-4B IT, OLMo-7B, Gemma 4B, Muse Spark 1.1, and Claude Opus 4.8. Each prompt is evaluated under all three mapping interventions.
